<p class="mw-empty-elt">
</p>

<p><b>Catherine of Siena</b>, TOSD (<a href="/wiki/Italian_(language)" class="mw-redirect" title="Italian (language)">Italian</a>: <i>Caterina da Siena</i>; 25 March 1347 – 29 April 1380) was an Italian  member of the <a href="/wiki/Third_Order_of_Saint_Dominic" title="Third Order of Saint Dominic">Third Order of Saint Dominic</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman Catholic Church">Roman Catholic  Church</a>. She was a mystic, activist, and author who had a great influence on <a href="/wiki/Italian_literature" title="Italian literature">Italian literature</a> and on the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic Church</a>. Canonized in 1461, she is also a <a href="/wiki/Doctor_of_the_Church" title="Doctor of the Church">Doctor of the Church</a>.
</p><p>Born and raised in <a href="/wiki/Siena" title="Siena">Siena</a>, she wanted from an early age to devote herself to <a href="/wiki/God" title="God">God</a>, against the will of her parents. She joined the "<a href="/wiki/Mantellate_Sisters" class="mw-redirect" title="Mantellate Sisters">mantellates</a>", a group of pious women, primarily widows, informally devoted to Dominican spirituality.<sup id="cite_ref-cloisteringcatherine_3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cloisteringcatherine-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> Her influence with <a href="/wiki/Pope_Gregory_XI" title="Pope Gregory XI">Pope Gregory XI</a> played a role in his 1376 decision to leave <a href="/wiki/Avignon" title="Avignon">Avignon</a> for <a href="/wiki/Rome" title="Rome">Rome</a>. The Pope then sent Catherine to <a href="/wiki/War_of_the_Eight_Saints" title="War of the Eight Saints"> negotiate peace</a> with <a href="/wiki/Florence" title="Florence">Florence</a>. After Gregory XI's death (March 1378) and the conclusion of peace (July 1378), she returned to Siena. She dictated to secretaries her set of spiritual treatises <i>The Dialogue of Divine Providence</i>. The <a href="/wiki/Western_Schism" title="Western Schism"> Great Schism of the West</a> led Catherine of Siena to go to Rome with the pope. She sent numerous letters to princes and cardinals to promote obedience to <a href="/wiki/Pope_Urban_VI" title="Pope Urban VI">Pope Urban VI</a> and to defend what she calls the "vessel of the Church". She died on 29 April 1380, exhausted by her rigorous fasting. Urban VI celebrated her funeral and burial in the <a href="/wiki/Basilica_of_Santa_Maria_sopra_Minerva" class="mw-redirect" title="Basilica of Santa Maria sopra Minerva">Basilica of Santa Maria sopra Minerva</a> in Rome.
</p><p>Devotion around Catherine of Siena developed rapidly after her death. <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_II" title="Pope Pius II">Pope Pius II</a> canonized her in 1461; she was declared a <a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">patron saint</a> of Rome in 1866 by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_IX" title="Pope Pius IX">Pope Pius IX</a>, and of <a href="/wiki/Italy" title="Italy">Italy</a> (together with <a href="/wiki/Francis_of_Assisi" title="Francis of Assisi">Francis of Assisi</a>) in 1939 by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_XII" title="Pope Pius XII">Pope Pius XII</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Vatican_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Vatican-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Vatican1_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Vatican1-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Vatican2_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Vatican2-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Vatican3_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Vatican3-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> She was the second woman  to be declared a "doctor of the Church," on 4 October 1970 by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Paul_VI" title="Pope Paul VI">Pope Paul VI</a> – only days after <a href="/wiki/Teresa_of_%C3%81vila" title="Teresa of Ávila">Teresa of Ávila</a>. In 1999 <a href="/wiki/Pope_John_Paul_II" title="Pope John Paul II">Pope John Paul II</a> proclaimed her a <a href="/wiki/Patron_Saint_of_Europe" class="mw-redirect" title="Patron Saint of Europe"> (co-)patron saint of Europe</a>.
</p><p>Catherine of Siena is one of the outstanding figures of medieval Catholicism, by the strong influence she has had in the history of the papacy and her extensive authorship.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> She was behind the return of the Pope from Avignon to Rome, and then carried out many missions entrusted to her by the pope, something quite rare for a woman in the <a href="/wiki/Middle_Ages" title="Middle Ages">Middle Ages</a>. Her <i>Dialogue</i>, hundreds of letters, and dozens of prayers, also give her a prominent place in the history of Italian literature.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Life">Life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Catherine_of_Siena&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:House_Catherine_Siena_Apr_2008.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ca/House_Catherine_Siena_Apr_2008.jpg/220px-House_Catherine_Siena_Apr_2008.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="109" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ca/House_Catherine_Siena_Apr_2008.jpg/330px-House_Catherine_Siena_Apr_2008.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ca/House_Catherine_Siena_Apr_2008.jpg/440px-House_Catherine_Siena_Apr_2008.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3392" data-file-height="1680" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:House_Catherine_Siena_Apr_2008.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The house of Saint Catherine in <a href="/wiki/Siena" title="Siena">Siena</a></div></div></div>
<p>Caterina di Jacopo di Benincasa was born on 25 March 1347 (shortly before the <a href="/wiki/Black_Death" title="Black Death">Black Death</a> ravaged Europe) in <a href="/wiki/Siena" title="Siena">Siena</a>, <a href="/wiki/Republic_of_Siena" title="Republic of Siena">Republic of Siena</a> (today <a href="/wiki/Italy" title="Italy">Italy</a>), to Lapa Piagenti, the daughter of a local poet, and Jacopo di Benincasa, a cloth dyer who ran his enterprise with the help of his sons.<sup id="cite_ref-Gardner_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Gardner-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> The house where Catherine grew up still exists. Lapa was about forty years old when she gave premature birth to twin daughters Catherine and Giovanna. She had already borne 22 children, but half of them had died. Giovanna was handed over to a <a href="/wiki/Wet-nurse" class="mw-redirect" title="Wet-nurse">wet-nurse</a> and died soon after. Catherine was nursed by her mother and developed into a healthy child. She was two years old when Lapa had her 25th child, another daughter named Giovanna.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESkårderud2008411_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESkårderud2008411-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> As a child Catherine was so merry that the family gave her the pet name of "Euphrosyne", which is Greek for "joy" and the name of an <a href="/wiki/Euphrosyne_of_Alexandria" title="Euphrosyne of Alexandria">Euphrosyne of Alexandria</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-crawley_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-crawley-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Catherine is said by her confessor and biographer <a href="/wiki/Raymond_of_Capua" title="Raymond of Capua">Raymond of Capua</a> <a href="/wiki/Dominican_Order" title="Dominican Order">O.P.</a>'s <i>Life</i> to have had her first vision of Christ when she was five or six years old: she and a brother were on the way home from visiting a married sister when she is said to have experienced a vision of <a href="/wiki/Christ_in_Majesty" title="Christ in Majesty">Christ seated in glory</a> with the <a href="/wiki/Apostles_in_the_New_Testament" title="Apostles in the New Testament">Apostles</a> <a href="/wiki/Saint_Peter" title="Saint Peter">Peter</a>, <a href="/wiki/Paul_of_Tarsus" class="mw-redirect" title="Paul of Tarsus">Paul</a>, and <a href="/wiki/John_the_Apostle" title="John the Apostle">John</a>. Raymond continues that at age seven, Catherine vowed to give her whole life to God.<sup id="cite_ref-crawley_12-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-crawley-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>When Catherine was sixteen, her older sister Bonaventura <a href="/wiki/Death_in_childbirth" class="mw-redirect" title="Death in childbirth">died in childbirth</a>; already anguished by this, Catherine soon learned that her parents wanted her to marry Bonaventura's widower. She was absolutely opposed and started a strict fast. She had learned this from Bonaventura, whose husband had been far from considerate but his wife had changed his attitude by refusing to eat until he showed better manners. Besides fasting, Catherine further disappointed her mother by cutting off her long hair as a protest against being overly encouraged to improve her appearance to attract a husband.<sup id="cite_ref-foley_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-foley-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:182px;"><a 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/26/%D0%A1%D0%B2_%D0%95%D0%BA%D0%B0%D1%82%D0%B5%D1%80%D0%B8%D0%BD%D0%B0_%D1%81%D0%B8%D0%B5%D0%BD%D1%81%D0%BA%D0%B0%D1%8F_%D0%9E%D1%80%D0%B0%D1%82%D0%BE%D1%80%D0%B8%D0%BE_%D0%A1%D0%B0%D0%BD%D1%82%D0%B0_%D0%9A%D0%B0%D1%82%D0%B5%D1%80%D0%B8%D0%BD%D0%B0_Neroccio.jpg/360px-%D0%A1%D0%B2_%D0%95%D0%BA%D0%B0%D1%82%D0%B5%D1%80%D0%B8%D0%BD%D0%B0_%D1%81%D0%B8%D0%B5%D0%BD%D1%81%D0%BA%D0%B0%D1%8F_%D0%9E%D1%80%D0%B0%D1%82%D0%BE%D1%80%D0%B8%D0%BE_%D0%A1%D0%B0%D0%BD%D1%82%D0%B0_%D0%9A%D0%B0%D1%82%D0%B5%D1%80%D0%B8%D0%BD%D0%B0_Neroccio.jpg href="/wiki/File:%D0%A1%D0%B2_%D0%95%D0%BA%D0%B0%D1%82%D0%B5%D1%80%D0%B8%D0%BD%D0%B0_%D1%81%D0%B8%D0%B5%D0%BD%D1%81%D0%BA%D0%B0%D1%8F_%D0%9E%D1%80%D0%B0%D1%82%D0%BE%D1%80%D0%B8%D0%BE_%D0%A1%D0%B0%D0%BD%D1%82%D0%B0_%D0%9A%D0%B0%D1%82%D0%B5%D1%80%D0%B8%D0%BD%D0%B0_Neroccio.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Statuette" class="mw-redirect" title="Statuette">Statuette</a> by <a href="/wiki/Neroccio_di_Bartolomeo_de%27_Landi" title="Neroccio di Bartolomeo de&#39; Landi">Neroccio di Bartolomeo de' Landi</a>, (1475)</div></div></div> 
<p>Catherine would later advise Raymond of Capua to do during times of trouble what she did now as a teenager: "Build a cell inside your mind, from which you can never flee." In this inner cell she made her father into a representation of Christ, her mother into the <a href="/wiki/Blessed_Virgin_Mary" class="mw-redirect" title="Blessed Virgin Mary">Blessed Virgin Mary</a>, and her brothers into the <a href="/wiki/Apostles_in_the_New_Testament" title="Apostles in the New Testament">apostles</a>. Serving them humbly became an opportunity for spiritual growth. Catherine resisted the accepted course of marriage and motherhood on the one hand, or a <a href="/wiki/Nun" title="Nun">nun</a>'s veil on the other. She chose to live an active and prayerful life outside a convent's walls following the model of the Dominicans.<sup id="cite_ref-bellitto_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bellitto-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> Eventually her father gave up and permitted her to live as she pleased.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (April 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>A vision of <a href="/wiki/Saint_Dominic" title="Saint Dominic">Dominic de Guzmán</a> gave strength to Catherine, but her wish to join his order was no comfort to Lapa, who took her daughter with her to the baths in <a href="/wiki/Bagno_Vignoni" title="Bagno Vignoni">Bagno Vignoni</a> to improve her health. Catherine fell seriously ill with a violent rash, fever and pain, which conveniently made her mother accept her wish to join the "Mantellate", the local association of devout women.<sup id="cite_ref-Siena._Available_Means_2001_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Siena._Available_Means_2001-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> The Mantellate taught Catherine how to read, and she lived in almost total silence and solitude in the family home.<sup id="cite_ref-Siena._Available_Means_2001_16-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Siena._Available_Means_2001-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Her custom of giving away clothing and food without asking anyone's permission cost her family significantly, but she requested nothing for herself. By staying in their midst, she could live out her rejection of them more strongly. She did not want their food, referring to the table laid for her in Heaven with her real family.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESkårderud2008412–413_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESkårderud2008412–413-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> 
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Giovanni_di_Paolo_The_Mystic_Marriage_of_Saint_Catherine_of_Siena.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/77/Giovanni_di_Paolo_The_Mystic_Marriage_of_Saint_Catherine_of_Siena.jpg/220px-Giovanni_di_Paolo_The_Mystic_Marriage_of_Saint_Catherine_of_Siena.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="221" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/77/Giovanni_di_Paolo_The_Mystic_Marriage_of_Saint_Catherine_of_Siena.jpg/330px-Giovanni_di_Paolo_The_Mystic_Marriage_of_Saint_Catherine_of_Siena.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/77/Giovanni_di_Paolo_The_Mystic_Marriage_of_Saint_Catherine_of_Siena.jpg/440px-Giovanni_di_Paolo_The_Mystic_Marriage_of_Saint_Catherine_of_Siena.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3639" data-file-height="3660" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Giovanni_di_Paolo_The_Mystic_Marriage_of_Saint_Catherine_of_Siena.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Giovanni_di_Paolo" title="Giovanni di Paolo">Giovanni di Paolo</a>, <i>The Mystic Marriage of Saint Catherine of Siena</i></div></div></div>
<p>According to <a href="/wiki/Raymond_of_Capua" title="Raymond of Capua">Raymond of Capua</a>, at the age of twenty-one (c. 1368), Catherine experienced what she described in her letters as a "<a href="/wiki/Bridal_theology" title="Bridal theology">Mystical Marriage</a>" with <a href="/wiki/Jesus" title="Jesus">Jesus</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERaymond_of_Capua200399–101_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERaymond_of_Capua200399–101-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> later a popular subject in art as the <i><a href="/wiki/Mystic_marriage_of_Saint_Catherine" class="mw-redirect" title="Mystic marriage of Saint Catherine">Mystic marriage of Saint Catherine</a></i>. <a href="/wiki/Caroline_Walker_Bynum" title="Caroline Walker Bynum">Caroline Walker Bynum</a> explains one surprising and controversial aspect of this marriage that occurs both in artistic representations of the event and in some early accounts of her life: "Underlining the extent to which the marriage was a fusion with Christ's physicality [...] Catherine received, not the ring of gold and jewels that her biographer reports in his bowdlerized version, but the ring of <a href="/wiki/Holy_Prepuce" title="Holy Prepuce">Christ's foreskin</a>."<sup id="cite_ref-Bynum_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bynum-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Catherine herself mentions the foreskin-as-wedding ring motif in one of her letters (#221), equating the wedding ring of a virgin with a foreskin; she typically claimed that her own wedding ring to Christ was simply invisible.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> She wrote in a letter (to encourage a nun who seems to have been undergoing a prolonged period of spiritual trial and torment): "Bathe in the blood of Christ crucified. See that you don't look for or want anything but the crucified, as a true bride ransomed by the blood of Christ crucified – for that is my wish. You see very well that you are a bride and that he has espoused you – you and everyone else – and not with a ring of silver but with a ring of his own flesh. Look at the tender little child who on the eighth day, when he was circumcised, gave up just so much flesh as to make a tiny circlet of a ring!"<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> Raymond of Capua also records that she was told by Christ to leave her withdrawn life and enter the public life of the world.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERaymond_of_Capua2003105–107_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERaymond_of_Capua2003105–107-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> Catherine rejoined her family and began helping the ill and the poor, where she took care of them in hospitals or homes. Her early pious activities in Siena attracted a group of followers, women and men, who gathered around her.<sup id="cite_ref-Gardner_10-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Gardner-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As social and political tensions mounted in Siena, Catherine found herself drawn to intervene in wider politics. She made her first journey to <a href="/wiki/Florence" title="Florence">Florence</a> in 1374, probably to be interviewed by the Dominican authorities at the General Chapter held in Florence in May 1374, though this is disputed (if she was interviewed, then the absence of later evidence suggests she was deemed sufficiently orthodox).<sup id="cite_ref-foley_14-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-foley-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> It seems that at this time she acquired Raymond of Capua as her confessor and spiritual director.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENoffke19805_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENoffke19805-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After this visit, she began travelling with her followers throughout northern and central Italy advocating reform of the clergy and advising people that repentance and renewal could be done through "the total love for God."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHollisterBennett2002342_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHollisterBennett2002342-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> In Pisa, in 1375, she used what influence she had to sway that city and <a href="/wiki/Lucca" title="Lucca">Lucca</a> away from alliance with the anti-papal league whose force was gaining momentum and strength. She also lent her enthusiasm toward promoting the launch of a new crusade. It was in Pisa in 1375 that, according to Raymond of Capua's biography, she received the <a href="/wiki/Stigmata" title="Stigmata">stigmata</a> (visible, at Catherine's request, only to herself).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENoffke19805_24-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENoffke19805-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

<li><a href="/wiki/Fatima_(2020_film)" title="Fatima (2020 film)">Fatima</a></li></ul></div></div></td>
</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"/><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1063604349">.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:inline;font-size:88%;font-weight:normal}.mw-parser-output .navbar-collapse{float:left;text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .navbar-boxtext{word-spacing:0}.mw-parser-output .navbar ul{display:inline-block;white-space:nowrap;line-height:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::before{margin-right:-0.125em;content:"[ "}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::after{margin-left:-0.125em;content:" ]"}.mw-parser-output .navbar li{word-spacing:-0.125em}.mw-parser-output .navbar a>span,.mw-parser-output .navbar a>abbr{text-decoration:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-mini abbr{font-variant:small-caps;border-bottom:none;text-decoration:none;cursor:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-full{font-size:114%;margin:0 7em}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-mini{font-size:114%;margin:0 4em}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Christian_mysticism" title="Template:Christian mysticism"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Christian_mysticism" title="Template talk:Christian mysticism"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Christian_mysticism&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>Physical travel was not the only way in which Catherine made her views known. From 1375<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENoffke19805_24-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENoffke19805-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> onward, she began dictating letters to scribes.<sup id="cite_ref-Siena._Available_Means_2001_16-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Siena._Available_Means_2001-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> These letters were intended to reach men and women of her circle, increasingly widening her audience to include figures in authority as she begged for peace between the republics and principalities of Italy and for the return of the <a href="/wiki/Papacy" class="mw-redirect" title="Papacy">Papacy</a> from <a href="/wiki/Avignon_Papacy" title="Avignon Papacy">Avignon</a> to <a href="/wiki/Rome" title="Rome">Rome</a>. She carried on a long correspondence with <a href="/wiki/Pope_Gregory_XI" title="Pope Gregory XI">Pope Gregory XI</a>, asking him to reform the <a href="/wiki/Clergy" title="Clergy">clergy</a> and the administration of the <a href="/wiki/Papal_States" title="Papal States">Papal States</a>.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (April 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Toward the end of 1375, she returned to Siena, to assist a young political prisoner, Niccolò di Tuldo, at his execution.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENoffke19805_24-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENoffke19805-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> In June 1376 Catherine went to <a href="/wiki/Avignon" title="Avignon">Avignon</a> as ambassador of the <a href="/wiki/Republic_of_Florence" title="Republic of Florence">Republic of Florence</a> to make peace with the Papal States (on 31 March 1376 Gregory XI had placed Florence under interdict). She was unsuccessful and was disowned by the Florentine leaders, who sent ambassadors to negotiate on their own terms as soon as Catherine's work had paved the way for them.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENoffke19805_24-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENoffke19805-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> Catherine sent an appropriately scorching letter back to Florence in response.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> While in Avignon, Catherine also tried to convince Pope Gregory XI, the last <a href="/wiki/Avignon_Papacy" title="Avignon Papacy">Avignon Pope</a>, to return to Rome.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHollisterBennett2002343_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHollisterBennett2002343-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> Gregory did indeed return his administration to Rome in January 1377; to what extent this was due to Catherine's influence is a topic of much modern debate.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Catherine returned to Siena and spent the early months of 1377 founding a women's monastery of strict observance outside the city in the old fortress of Belcaro.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENoffke19806_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENoffke19806-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> She spent the rest of 1377 at Rocca d'Orcia, about twenty miles from Siena, on a local mission of peace-making and preaching. During this period, in autumn 1377, she had the experience which led to the writing of her <i>Dialogue</i> and learned to write, although she still seems to have chiefly relied upon her secretaries for her correspondence.<sup id="cite_ref-Gardner_10-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Gardner-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Late in 1377 or early in 1378 Catherine again travelled to Florence, at the order of Gregory XI,<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> to seek peace between Florence and Rome. Following Gregory's death in <a href="/wiki/Western_Schism" title="Western Schism">March 1378 riots</a>, the revolts of the <a href="/wiki/Ciompi" class="mw-redirect" title="Ciompi">Ciompi</a>, broke out in Florence on 18 June, and in the ensuing violence she was nearly assassinated. Eventually, in July 1378, peace was agreed between Florence and Rome; Catherine returned quietly to Florence.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (April 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>In late November 1378, with the outbreak of the <a href="/wiki/Western_Schism" title="Western Schism">Western Schism</a>, the new Pope, <a href="/wiki/Urban_VI" class="mw-redirect" title="Urban VI">Urban VI</a>, summoned her to Rome. She stayed at Pope Urban VI's court and tried to convince nobles and cardinals of his legitimacy, both meeting with individuals at court and writing letters to persuade others.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENoffke19806_30-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENoffke19806-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>For many years she had accustomed herself to a rigorous abstinence.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> She received the Holy <a href="/wiki/Eucharist" title="Eucharist">Eucharist</a> almost daily. This extreme fasting appeared unhealthy in the eyes of the clergy and her own sisterhood. Her confessor, Raymond, ordered her to eat properly. But Catherine replied that she was unable to, describing her inability to eat as an <i>infermità</i> (illness). From the beginning of 1380, Catherine could neither eat nor swallow water. On 26 February she lost the use of her legs.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENoffke19806_30-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENoffke19806-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Catherine died in Rome, on 29 April 1380, at the age of thirty-three,<sup id="cite_ref-dictionary_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dictionary-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> having eight days earlier suffered a massive stroke which paralyzed her from the waist down. Her last words were, "Father, into Your Hands I commend my soul and my spirit."<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Sources_on_her_life">Sources on her life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Catherine_of_Siena&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Sources on her life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Bruges_Public_Library_MS767_v.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7b/Bruges_Public_Library_MS767_v.jpg/220px-Bruges_Public_Library_MS767_v.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="303" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7b/Bruges_Public_Library_MS767_v.jpg/330px-Bruges_Public_Library_MS767_v.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7b/Bruges_Public_Library_MS767_v.jpg/440px-Bruges_Public_Library_MS767_v.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3587" data-file-height="4941" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Bruges_Public_Library_MS767_v.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Leaf from an anonymous French translation of the Legenda Major by <a href="/wiki/Raymond_of_Capua" title="Raymond of Capua">Raymond of Capua</a> showing the vision of the mystic wedding of Catherine and Christ. (<a href="/wiki/Bruges_Public_Library" title="Bruges Public Library">Bruges Public Library</a>, MS 767)</div></div></div>
<p>There is some internal evidence of Catherine's personality, teaching and work in her nearly four hundred letters, her <i>Dialogue</i>, and her prayers.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Much detail about her life has also, however, been drawn from the various sources written shortly after her death to promote her cult and canonization. Though much of the material is heavily <a href="/wiki/Hagiography" title="Hagiography">hagiographic</a>, it has been an important source for historians seeking to reconstruct Catherine's life. Various sources are particularly important, especially the works of Raymond of Capua, who was Catherine's spiritual director and close friend from 1374 to her death and himself became <a href="/wiki/Master_of_the_Order_of_Preachers" title="Master of the Order of Preachers">Master General</a> of the Order in 1380. Raymond wrote what is known as the <i>Legenda Major</i>, his <i>Life</i> of Catherine which was completed in 1395, fifteen years after Catherine's death.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Another important work written after Catherine's death was <i>Libellus de Supplemento</i> (<i>Little Supplement Book</i>), written between 1412 and 1418 by Tommaso d'Antonio Nacci da Siena (commonly called Thomas of Siena, or Tommaso Caffarini); the work is an expansion of Raymond's <i>Legenda Major</i> making heavy use of the notes of Catherine's first confessor, Tommaso della Fonte, that do not survive anywhere else. Caffarini later published a more compact account of Catherine's life, the <i>Legenda Minor</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>From 1411 onward, Caffarini also coordinated the compiling of the <i>Processus</i> of Venice, the set of documents submitted as part of the process of <a href="/wiki/Canonisation" class="mw-redirect" title="Canonisation">canonisation</a> of Catherine, which provides testimony from nearly all of Catherine's disciples. There is also an anonymous piece, "Miracoli della Beata Caterina" (<i>Miracle of Blessed Catherine</i>), written by an anonymous Florentine. A few other relevant pieces survive.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENoffke19802_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENoffke19802-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Works">Works</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Catherine_of_Siena&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Works">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Caterina_-_Libro_della_divina_dottrina,_circa_1475_-_2367969.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d6/Caterina_-_Libro_della_divina_dottrina%2C_circa_1475_-_2367969.jpg/220px-Caterina_-_Libro_della_divina_dottrina%2C_circa_1475_-_2367969.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="340" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d6/Caterina_-_Libro_della_divina_dottrina%2C_circa_1475_-_2367969.jpg/330px-Caterina_-_Libro_della_divina_dottrina%2C_circa_1475_-_2367969.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d6/Caterina_-_Libro_della_divina_dottrina%2C_circa_1475_-_2367969.jpg/440px-Caterina_-_Libro_della_divina_dottrina%2C_circa_1475_-_2367969.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2184" data-file-height="3379" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Caterina_-_Libro_della_divina_dottrina,_circa_1475_-_2367969.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><i> Libro della divina dottrina</i> (commonly known as <i>The Dialogue of Divine Providence</i>), c.1475</div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:L%27epistole_della_serafica_vergine_s._Caterina_da_Siena.tif" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/14/L%27epistole_della_serafica_vergine_s._Caterina_da_Siena.tif/lossy-page1-220px-L%27epistole_della_serafica_vergine_s._Caterina_da_Siena.tif.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="319" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/14/L%27epistole_della_serafica_vergine_s._Caterina_da_Siena.tif/lossy-page1-330px-L%27epistole_della_serafica_vergine_s._Caterina_da_Siena.tif.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/14/L%27epistole_della_serafica_vergine_s._Caterina_da_Siena.tif/lossy-page1-440px-L%27epistole_della_serafica_vergine_s._Caterina_da_Siena.tif.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3008" data-file-height="4368" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:L%27epistole_della_serafica_vergine_s._Caterina_da_Siena.tif" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><i>L'epistole della serafica vergine s. Caterina da Siena</i> (1721)</div></div></div>
<p>Three genres of work by Catherine survive:
</p>
<ul><li>Her major treatise is <i>The Dialogue of Divine Providence</i>. This had probably begun in October 1377 and was certainly finished by November 1378. Contemporaries of Catherine are united in asserting that much of the book was dictated while Catherine was in ecstasy, though it also seems possible that Catherine herself may then have re-edited many passages in the book.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENoffke198013_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENoffke198013-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> It is a dialogue between a soul who "rises up" to God and God himself.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (April 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup></li>
<li>Catherine's letters are considered one of the great works of early Tuscan literature. Many of these were dictated, although she herself learned to write in 1377; 382 have survived. In her letters to the Pope, she often addressed him affectionately simply as <i>Babbo</i> ("Daddy"), instead of the formal form of address "Your Holiness".<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> Other correspondents include her various confessors, among them <a href="/wiki/Raymond_of_Capua" title="Raymond of Capua">Raymond of Capua</a>, the kings of France and Hungary, the infamous mercenary <a href="/wiki/John_Hawkwood" title="John Hawkwood">John Hawkwood</a>, the Queen of Naples, members of the <a href="/wiki/Visconti_of_Milan" title="Visconti of Milan">Visconti family</a> of Milan, and numerous religious figures.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> Approximately one third of her letters are to women.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (April 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup></li>
<li>Twenty-six prayers of Catherine of Siena also survive, mostly composed in the last eighteen months of her life.</li></ul>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/University_of_Alcal%C3%A1" title="University of Alcalá">University of Alcalá</a> conserves a unique handwritten Spanish manuscript, while other available texts are printed copies collected by the <a href="/wiki/National_Library_of_France" class="mw-redirect" title="National Library of France">National Library of France</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-10.1093/fh/crt047_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10.1093/fh/crt047-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Theology">Theology</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Catherine_of_Siena&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Theology">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Catherine's theology can be described as <a href="/wiki/Christian_mysticism" title="Christian mysticism">mystical</a>, and was employed toward practical ends for her own spiritual life or those of others.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> She used the language of medieval <a href="/wiki/Scholastic_philosophy" class="mw-redirect" title="Scholastic philosophy">scholastic philosophy</a> to elaborate her experiential mysticism.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> Interested mainly with achieving an incorporeal union with God, Catherine practiced extreme fasting and <a href="/wiki/Ascetical_theology" title="Ascetical theology">asceticism</a>, eventually to the extent of living solely on the Eucharist every day.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> For Catherine, this practice was the means to fully realize her <a href="/wiki/Agape" title="Agape">love of Christ</a> in her mystical experience, with a large proportion of her <a href="/wiki/Religious_ecstasy" title="Religious ecstasy">ecstatic visions</a> relating to the consumption or rejection of food during her life.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> She viewed Christ as a "bridge" between the soul and God and transmitted that idea, along with her other teachings, in her book The Dialogue.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> The Dialogue is highly systematic and explanatory in its presentation of her mystical ideas; however, these ideas themselves are not so much based on reason or logic as they are based in her ecstatic mystical experience.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In one of her letters she sent to her confessor, <a href="/wiki/Raymund_of_Capua" class="mw-redirect" title="Raymund of Capua">Raymund of Capua</a>, she recorded this revelation from her conversation with Christ, in which he said: <i>"Do you know what you are to Me, and what I am to you, my daughter? I am He who is, you are she who is not"</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> This mystical concept of God as the wellspring of being is seen in the works and ideas of <a href="/wiki/Aquinas" class="mw-redirect" title="Aquinas">Aquinas</a><sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> and can be seen as a simplistic rendering of <a href="/wiki/Apotheosis" title="Apotheosis">apotheosis</a> and a more rudimentary form of the <a href="/wiki/Divine_simplicity" title="Divine simplicity">doctrine of divine simplicity</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> She describes God in her work, '<i>the Dialogues'</i>, as a <i>"sea, in which we are the fish"</i>, the point being that the relationship between God and man should not be seen as man contending against the Divine and vice versa, but as God being the endless being that supports all things.<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to the writings attributed to Catherine, she had, in 1377, a vision in which the Virgin confirmed to her a thesis supported by the Dominican order, to which Catherine belonged: the Virgin said that she had been conceived <i>with</i> the original sin. The Virgin thus contradicted the future dogma of the <a href="/wiki/Immaculate_Conception" title="Immaculate Conception">Immaculate Conception</a>. Cardinal Lambertini (later <a href="/wiki/Pope_Benedict_XIV" title="Pope Benedict XIV">Pope Benedict XIV</a>), in his treatise <i>De servorum Dei beatificatione et de beatorum canonizatione</i>, 1734-1738,<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> cites theologians who believed that Catherine's directors or editors had falsified her words; he also cites Father Lancicius,<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> who believed that Catherine had made a mistake as a result of preconceived ideas.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Veneration">Veneration</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Catherine_of_Siena&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Veneration">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Feast_of_Saints_Francis_and_Catherine" title="Feast of Saints Francis and Catherine">Feast of Saints Francis and Catherine</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Caterina_sopra_Minerva.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0d/Caterina_sopra_Minerva.jpg/220px-Caterina_sopra_Minerva.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="146" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0d/Caterina_sopra_Minerva.jpg/330px-Caterina_sopra_Minerva.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0d/Caterina_sopra_Minerva.jpg/440px-Caterina_sopra_Minerva.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1010" data-file-height="670" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Caterina_sopra_Minerva.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Sarcophagus" title="Sarcophagus">Sarcophagus</a> of Catherine beneath the <a href="/wiki/High_Altar" class="mw-redirect" title="High Altar">High Altar</a> of Santa Maria sopra Minerva, Rome</div></div></div>
<p>She was buried in the (Roman) cemetery of <a href="/wiki/Santa_Maria_sopra_Minerva" title="Santa Maria sopra Minerva">Santa Maria sopra Minerva</a> which lies near the <a href="/wiki/Pantheon,_Rome" title="Pantheon, Rome">Pantheon</a>. After miracles were reported to take place at her grave, Raymond moved her inside the <a href="/wiki/Basilica" title="Basilica">Basilica</a> of Santa Maria sopra Minerva, where she lies to this day.<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Cappella_di_santa_caterina,_01.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cc/Cappella_di_santa_caterina%2C_01.jpg/220px-Cappella_di_santa_caterina%2C_01.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="314" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cc/Cappella_di_santa_caterina%2C_01.jpg/330px-Cappella_di_santa_caterina%2C_01.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cc/Cappella_di_santa_caterina%2C_01.jpg/440px-Cappella_di_santa_caterina%2C_01.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2188" data-file-height="3126" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Cappella_di_santa_caterina,_01.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The Chapel of Saint Catherine, Basilica of San Domenico in Siena</div></div></div>
<p>Her head however, was parted from her body and inserted in a gilt bust of <a href="/wiki/Bronze" title="Bronze">bronze</a>. This bust was later taken to Siena, and carried through that city in a procession to the Dominican church. Behind the bust walked Lapa, Catherine's mother, who lived until she was 89 years old. By then she had seen the end of the wealth and the happiness of her family, and followed most of her children and several of her grandchildren to the grave. She helped Raymond of Capua write his biography of her daughter, and said, "I think God has laid my soul athwart in my body, so that it can't get out."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESkårderud2008&quot;Jeg_tror_at_Gud_har_gjort_det_slik_at_sjelen_ligger_på_tvers_i_kroppen_min_og_ikke_kan_komme_ut.&quot;_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESkårderud2008&quot;Jeg_tror_at_Gud_har_gjort_det_slik_at_sjelen_ligger_på_tvers_i_kroppen_min_og_ikke_kan_komme_ut.&quot;-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> The incorrupt head and thumb were entombed in the <a href="/wiki/Basilica_of_San_Domenico_(Siena)" class="mw-redirect" title="Basilica of San Domenico (Siena)">Basilica of San Domenico</a> at Siena, where they remain.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_II" title="Pope Pius II">Pope Pius II</a>, himself from Siena, <a href="/wiki/Canonized" class="mw-redirect" title="Canonized">canonized</a> Catherine on 29 June 1461.<sup id="cite_ref-beckwith_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-beckwith-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 4 October 1970, <a href="/wiki/Pope_Paul_VI" title="Pope Paul VI">Pope Paul VI</a> named Catherine a <a href="/wiki/Doctor_of_the_Church" title="Doctor of the Church">Doctor of the Church</a>;<sup id="cite_ref-Vatican1_6-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Vatican1-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> this title was almost simultaneously given to <a href="/wiki/Teresa_of_%C3%81vila" title="Teresa of Ávila">Teresa of Ávila</a> (27 September 1970),<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> making them the first women to receive this honour.<sup id="cite_ref-beckwith_63-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-beckwith-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Initially however, her <a href="/wiki/Feast_day" class="mw-redirect" title="Feast day">feast day</a> was not included in the <a href="/wiki/General_Roman_Calendar" title="General Roman Calendar">General Roman Calendar</a>. When it was added in 1597, it was put on the day of her death, 29 April; however, because this conflicted with the feast of Saint <a href="/wiki/Peter_of_Verona" title="Peter of Verona">Peter of Verona</a> which also fell on 29 April, Catherine's feast day was moved in 1628 to the new date of 30 April.<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> In the <a href="/wiki/Mysterii_Paschalis" title="Mysterii Paschalis">1969 revision</a> of the calendar, it was decided to leave the celebration of the feast of St Peter of Verona to local calendars, because he was not as well known worldwide, and Catherine's feast was restored to 29 April.<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Catherine is <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Church_of_England)" title="Calendar of saints (Church of England)">remembered</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_England" title="Church of England">Church of England</a> and in the <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Episcopal_Church)" title="Calendar of saints (Episcopal Church)">Episcopal Church</a> on 29 April.<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Patronage">Patronage</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Catherine_of_Siena&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Patronage">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In his decree of 13 April 1866, <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_IX" title="Pope Pius IX">Pope Pius IX</a> declared Catherine of Siena to be a co-patroness of Rome. On 18 June 1939 <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_XII" title="Pope Pius XII">Pope Pius XII</a> named her a joint <a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">patron saint</a> of <a href="/wiki/Italy" title="Italy">Italy</a> along with <a href="/wiki/Francis_of_Assisi" title="Francis of Assisi">Francis of Assisi</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Vatican_5-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Vatican-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 1 October 1999, <a href="/wiki/Pope_John_Paul_II" title="Pope John Paul II">Pope John Paul II</a> made her one of Europe's <a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">patron saints</a>, along with <a href="/wiki/Edith_Stein" title="Edith Stein">Teresa Benedicta of the Cross</a> and <a href="/wiki/Bridget_of_Sweden" title="Bridget of Sweden">Bridget of Sweden</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Vatican2_7-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Vatican2-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Vatican3_8-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Vatican3-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> She is also the patroness of the historically Catholic American woman's fraternity, <a href="/wiki/Theta_Phi_Alpha" title="Theta Phi Alpha">Theta Phi Alpha</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Severed_head">Severed head</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Catherine_of_Siena&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Severed head">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The people of Siena wished to have Catherine's body. A story is told of a miracle whereby they were partially successful: knowing that they could not smuggle her whole body out of Rome, they decided to take only her head which they placed in a bag. When stopped by the Roman guards, they prayed to Catherine to help them, confident that she would rather have her body (or at least part thereof) in Siena. When they opened the bag to show the guards, it appeared no longer to hold her head but to be full of rose petals.<sup id="cite_ref-AO_70-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-AO-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Catherine_of_Siena&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Legacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Catherine ranks high among the mystics and spiritual writers of the Catholic Church.<sup id="cite_ref-foley_14-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-foley-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> She remains a greatly respected figure for her spiritual writings, and political boldness to "speak truth to power"—it being exceptional for a woman, in her time period, to have had such influence in politics and on world history.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (April 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Main_sanctuaries">Main sanctuaries</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Catherine_of_Siena&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Main sanctuaries">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>The main churches in honor of Catherine of Siena are:
</p>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Santa_Maria_sopra_Minerva" title="Santa Maria sopra Minerva">Santa Maria sopra Minerva</a> in Rome: place where her body is preserved.<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Basilica_of_San_Domenico,_Siena" title="Basilica of San Domenico, Siena">Basilica of San Domenico</a> in Siena: in this church the incorrupt head of Catherine of Siena is preserved.<sup id="cite_ref-AO_70-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-AO-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/w/index.php?title=Shrine_of_Saint_Catherine_(Siena)&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Shrine of Saint Catherine (Siena) (page does not exist)">Shrine of Saint Catherine</a><span class="noprint" style="font-size:85%; font-style: normal;">&#160;&#91;<a href="https://it.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santuario_di_Santa_Caterina_(Siena)" class="extiw" title="it:Santuario di Santa Caterina (Siena)">it</a>&#93;</span> in Siena: complex of religious buildings built around the birthplace of Catherine.<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Images">Images</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Catherine_of_Siena&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Images">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>

<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Bibliography">Bibliography</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Catherine_of_Siena&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Bibliography">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p><b>Modern editions and English translations</b>
</p>
<ul><li>The Italian critical edition of the <i>Dialogue</i> is Catherine of Siena, <i>Il Dialogo della divina Provvidenza: ovvero Libro della divina dottrina</i>, 2nd ed., ed. Giuliana Cavallini (Siena: Cantagalli, 1995). [1st edn, 1968] [Cavallini demonstrated that the standard division of the <i>Dialogue</i> in into four treatises entitled the 'Treatise on Discretion', 'On Prayer', 'On Providence', and 'On Obedience', was in fact a result of a misreading of the text in the 1579 edition of the <i>Dialogue</i>. Modern editors and translators, including <a href="#CITEREFNoffke1980">Noffke (1980)</a>, have followed Cavallini in rejecting this fourfold division.]</li>
<li>The Italian critical edition of the 26 <i>Prayers</i> is Catherine of Siena, <i>Le Orazioni</i>, ed. Giuliana Cavallini (Rome: Cateriniane, 1978)</li>
<li>The most recent Italian critical edition of the Letters is Antonio Volpato, ed, <i>Le lettere di Santa Caterina da Siena: l'edizione di Eugenio Duprè Theseider e i nuovi problemi</i>, (2002)</li></ul>
<p>English translations of The <i>Dialogue</i> include:
</p>
<ul><li><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://books.google.com/books?id=bsGXhNZyUSgC"><i>The Dialogue</i></a>, trans. Suzanne Noffke, O.P. Paulist Press (Classics of Western Spirituality), 1980.</li>
<li><i>The Dialogue of St. Catherine of Siena</i>, <a href="/wiki/TAN_Books" title="TAN Books">TAN Books</a> , 2009. <style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1133582631">.mw-parser-output cite.citation{font-style:inherit;word-wrap:break-word}.mw-parser-output .citation q{quotes:"\"""\"""'""'"}.mw-parser-output .citation:target{background-color:rgba(0,127,255,0.133)}.mw-parser-output .id-lock-free a,.mw-parser-output .citation .cs1-lock-free a{background:url("//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/6/65/Lock-green.svg")right 0.1em center/9px no-repeat}.mw-parser-output .id-lock-limited a,.mw-parser-output .id-lock-registration a,.mw-parser-output .citation .cs1-lock-limited a,.mw-parser-output .citation .cs1-lock-registration a{background:url("//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/d/d6/Lock-gray-alt-2.svg")right 0.1em center/9px no-repeat}.mw-parser-output .id-lock-subscription a,.mw-parser-output .citation .cs1-lock-subscription a{background:url("//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/a/aa/Lock-red-alt-2.svg")right 0.1em center/9px no-repeat}.mw-parser-output .cs1-ws-icon a{background:url("//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/4/4c/Wikisource-logo.svg")right 0.1em center/12px no-repeat}.mw-parser-output .cs1-code{color:inherit;background:inherit;border:none;padding:inherit}.mw-parser-output .cs1-hidden-error{display:none;color:#d33}.mw-parser-output .cs1-visible-error{color:#d33}.mw-parser-output .cs1-maint{display:none;color:#3a3;margin-left:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .cs1-format{font-size:95%}.mw-parser-output .cs1-kern-left{padding-left:0.2em}.mw-parser-output .cs1-kern-right{padding-right:0.2em}.mw-parser-output .citation .mw-selflink{font-weight:inherit}</style><a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-0-89555-149-8" title="Special:BookSources/978-0-89555-149-8">978-0-89555-149-8</a></li>
<li>Phyllis Hodgson and Gabriel M Liegey, eds., <i>The Orcherd of Syon</i>, (London; New York: Oxford UP, 1966) [A Middle English translation of the <i>Dialogo</i> from the early fifteenth century, first printed in 1519].</li></ul>
<p>The Letters are translated into English as:
</p>
<ul><li><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><cite id="CITEREFCatherine_of_Siena1988" class="citation book cs1">Catherine of Siena (1988).  Suzanne Noffke (ed.). <i>The Letters of St. Catherine of Siena</i>. Vol.&#160;4. Binghamton: Center for Medieval and Early Renaissance Studies, State University of New York at Binghamton. <a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-0-86698-036-4" title="Special:BookSources/978-0-86698-036-4"><bdi>978-0-86698-036-4</bdi></a>.</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=The+Letters+of+St.+Catherine+of+Siena&amp;rft.place=Binghamton&amp;rft.pub=Center+for+Medieval+and+Early+Renaissance+Studies%2C+State+University+of+New+York+at+Binghamton&amp;rft.date=1988&amp;rft.isbn=978-0-86698-036-4&amp;rft.au=Catherine+of+Siena&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ACatherine+of+Siena" class="Z3988"></span> (Republished as <i>The letters of Catherine of Siena</i>, 4 vols, trans Suzanne Noffke, (Tempe, AZ: Arizona Center for Medieval and Renaissance Studies, 2000–2008))</li></ul>
<p>The Prayers are translated into English as:
</p>
<ul><li><i>The Prayers of Catherine of Siena</i>, trans. Suzanne Noffke, 2nd edn 1983, (New York, 2001)</li></ul>
<p>Raymond of Capua's <i>Life</i> was translated into English in 1493 and 1609, and in Modern English is translated as:
</p>
<ul><li><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><cite id="CITEREFRaymond_of_Capua1980" class="citation book cs1"><a href="/wiki/Raymond_of_Capua" title="Raymond of Capua">Raymond of Capua</a> (1980).  Conleth Kearns (ed.). <i>The Life of Catherine of Siena</i>. Wilmington: Glazier. <a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-0-89453-151-4" title="Special:BookSources/978-0-89453-151-4"><bdi>978-0-89453-151-4</bdi></a>.</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=The+Life+of+Catherine+of+Siena&amp;rft.place=Wilmington&amp;rft.pub=Glazier&amp;rft.date=1980&amp;rft.isbn=978-0-89453-151-4&amp;rft.au=Raymond+of+Capua&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ACatherine+of+Siena" class="Z3988"></span></li></ul>
<p>Letter Excerpts translated into English:
</p>
<ul><li><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://catherineofsiena-spirituality.org/letters-illustrative-passages/"><i>Letter Excerpts</i></a>, translations by Diana L. Villegas, Ph.D. from the <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://centrostudicateriniani.it/it/santa-caterina-da-siena/scritti"><i>Volpato critical edition</i></a>.</li></ul>

</li>
<li id="cite_note-FOOTNOTESkårderud2008&quot;Jeg_tror_at_Gud_har_gjort_det_slik_at_sjelen_ligger_på_tvers_i_kroppen_min_og_ikke_kan_komme_ut.&quot;-59"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-FOOTNOTESkårderud2008&quot;Jeg_tror_at_Gud_har_gjort_det_slik_at_sjelen_ligger_på_tvers_i_kroppen_min_og_ikke_kan_komme_ut.&quot;_59-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFSkårderud2008">Skårderud 2008</a>, "Jeg tror at Gud har gjort det slik at sjelen ligger på tvers i kroppen min og ikke kan komme ut.".</span>
